Inspected: Water Quality data CP

  • Dataset identifier: doi:10.23719/1531692
  • Contents described: Field and laboratory data from groundwater monitoring wells and surface-water samples at a field-scale permeable reactive barrier.
  • Research context: Associated with a 2025 study of nitrate and ammonium remediation at a livestock feeding operation.
  • Available file: Water Quality Data CP.xlsx
  • Access and terms: Metadata labels access as public and links to the EPA ScienceHub license.
  • Metadata gaps: No observation period, geographic coverage, or data dictionary was provided. The listed modification date, August 14, 2024, is a metadata date—not a sampling date.
  • Inspection reference: Harvest record b2ce8834-96cf-4f8d-9cbb-3ed8a14baaed.

Caveat: I inspected the catalog metadata and file listing, not the spreadsheet contents; variable names, units, sampling dates, and measured values remain unverified.

Your score

D2 run 1 · 25.5 s · discovery

auto: pass

Question: Find housing affordability datasets. Explain which appear useful and what still needs checking.

Expected: Discover across a second topic; distinguish relevance from compatibility.

Agent answer

Most useful starting points

I searched Data.gov for “housing affordability” and inspected four promising records. CHAS looks strongest for local housing-needs analysis; HADS is more useful for historical household-level research. These judgments are based on metadata—not downloaded data.

Dataset Why it appears useful What still needs checking
Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS) — HUD, identifier HUD044 Custom Census tabulations describing housing problems and needs, particularly among low-income households. A good starting point for housing-assistance planning. Available geographic levels and observation periods; exact measures and denominators; download/API functionality. The catalog labels the resource “API,” but that functionality has not been verified. Access and reuse terms are unclear in the inspected record.
ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by County — HUD, ArcGIS item dbcc9ba4a4144a6d823b4d429d2b97c6 Most immediately promising for county comparisons and mapping. Describes household housing needs by income categories, including thresholds relative to HUD Area Median Family Income. Lists CSV, Excel, GIS downloads, and a REST service. The description specifies 2016–2020 ACS observations, not current conditions. Verify field definitions, geographic identifiers and coverage, missing/suppressed values, uncertainty documentation, and whether a newer release exists. This is a CHAS product, not independent corroboration of CHAS.
Housing Affordability Data System (HADS) — HUD, identifier HUD031 AHS-derived files with affordability classifications and housing-cost burden for owners and renters. Promising for historical analysis using consistent affordability measures. The metadata says 2013 is the latest public-use file, with 2015 onward available only as internal-use files. Confirm present availability, access restrictions, geographic detail, documentation, and comparability across years.
Seattle Mandatory Housing Affordability Zones — ArcGIS item cce94a62698640b59031ae35292acfe5 Useful for examining where Seattle’s affordable-housing development requirements may apply. Lists tabular and geospatial formats. This is policy geography, not a measure of household affordability or program outcomes. The publisher explicitly says it is not an official zoning map. Verify current legal applicability and effective dates with Seattle’s planning authority.

Recommendation

Start with county CHAS for geographic needs comparisons, or the main CHAS portal to find a suitable geography and release. Use HADS when historical household-level analysis is the priority.

Before analysis, confirm the affordability definition, population covered, observation period, geography, file accessibility, and reuse terms. Metadata modification or harvest dates do not establish data vintage. I have not fetched these distribution links or validated their contents.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct

Complete shortlist and checks-to-do; HADS access caveat and CHAS 2016–2020 vintage correctly attributed to metadata. Seattle zoning is not misrepresented as an affordability outcome.

This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov. Agent answer

Dataset Publisher Contents and coverage Source
GIS Features of the Geospatial Fabric for National Hydrologic Modeling USGS Vector features for region outlines, points of interest, stream-routing networks, and hydrologic response units. Covers the lower 48 states, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico, including Maryland. Dataset access · Identifier: USGS_581a162ee4b0bb36a4ca2e29

Caveats: The location filter matches catalog spatial extents—not individual feature geometries—so these are broad-coverage datasets intersecting Maryland, not Maryland-only extracts. Files and linked pages were not downloaded or tested. Neither inspected record specifies an observation period; metadata modification or harvest dates should not be treated as data vintage. This is a selection of results, not an exhaustive inventory.

auto: warn

Question: Can we treat a catalog harvest date as the observation year?

Expected: No; separate harvest, release and observation dates.

Agent answer

No. A catalog harvest date records when metadata was collected or refreshed in the catalog—not when the underlying observations were measured.

To establish the observation year, check the dataset’s:

  • Temporal coverage or reference-period fields
  • Data documentation defining the measurement period
  • Actual records, including year or date columns

A dataset harvested in 2024 could contain observations from 2022, multiple years, or another period entirely. The harvest date alone also does not establish the data’s release date or vintage. If the observation period is unclear, report it as unknown rather than infer it from harvesting.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Correct standalone explanation, but no source citation or live example. The automatic no-tool warning is not an environment failure; human reviewer may prefer a sourced explanation.

M3 run 1 · 10.7 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Can county unemployment rates be averaged to get the state rate?

Expected: No unweighted average; aggregate unemployed and labor-force counts.

Agent answer

Not with a simple average. County unemployment rates have different civilian labor-force denominators, so giving each county equal weight does not produce the state rate.

The appropriate aggregation is:

[ \text{State unemployment rate} =100\times\frac{\sum \text{county unemployed}}{\sum \text{county civilian labor force}} ]

This is equivalent to weighting county rates by their labor-force sizes—not by total population. Use consistent periods and definitions; rounded county rates may not reproduce the published state rate exactly.

For the registered Maryland data for 2022–2024, the definition is annual average, not seasonally adjusted, and the aggregation rule explicitly says to use summed unemployed and labor force rather than averaging county rates. Include Baltimore city alongside Maryland’s counties.

Source: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics—Maryland, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct

Grounded in the actual registry, with concise complete wording. No numerical comparison was necessary.

citationspassBLS source and identifier are explicitly cited.
limitationspassExplicit definition and time/adjustment context; no stale awaiting-review claim.

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Worcester County was the only Maryland county where population grew while the unemployment rate fell from 2022 to 2024. The comparison covered all 24 Maryland county-level jurisdictions, including Baltimore city, with no missing coverage.

County Population, 2022 Population, 2024 Population change Unemployment, 2022 Unemployment, 2024 Rate change
Worcester (FIPS 24047) 54,004 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Population figures are July 1 estimates, both from Census Vintage 2024. Unemployment figures are annual-average, not-seasonally-adjusted rates measuring unemployment as a share of the civilian labor force—not total population. These different time summaries support a descriptive comparison, not a causal conclusion.

Sources: Census county population estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (catalog ID DOL-BLS-3060-01; estimates may be revised).

Analysis evidence ID: 4158aa09148848b5a249dc69421d37ab.

Agent answer

Worcester County was the only Maryland county where population grew while the unemployment rate fell from 2022 to 2024. The comparison covered all 24 county-level jurisdictions, including Baltimore city, with no missing coverage.

County Population, 2022 → 2024 Population change Unemployment rate, 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester (FIPS 24047) 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

These different time summaries show an association, not that population growth caused unemployment to fall.

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Worcester County was the only Maryland county where population grew while the unemployment rate fell from 2022 to 2024. The comparison covered all 24 county-equivalents, including Baltimore city, with no missing coverage.

County Population, 2022 → 2024 Population change Unemployment rate, 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester (FIPS 24047) 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Population figures are July 1 estimates from Census Vintage 2024; unemployment rates are annual averages, not seasonally adjusted, expressed as a share of the civilian labor force. These are different temporal summaries, and their co-occurrence does not establish causation.

Sources: Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (identifier: DOL-BLS-3060-01). Estimates may be revised.

Analysis evidence ID: 2c1e2bd1208d42aca25af3e0094ba387.

A2 run 1 · 17.3 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Compare Baltimore city's population and unemployment in 2022 and 2024.

Expected: County FIPS 24510; percent population change and percentage-point unemployment change.

Agent answer

Baltimore city: 2022 vs. 2024

Baltimore city, Maryland (FIPS 24510), not Baltimore County.

Indicator 2022 2024 Change
Resident population 570,663 568,271 −0.42%
Unemployment rate 3.8% 4.3% +0.5 percentage points

Population declined slightly, while the unemployment rate rose. This descriptive comparison does not establish that either change caused the other.

Definitions and caveats: Population figures are July 1 estimates, both from Census Vintage 2024. Unemployment figures are annual averages, not seasonally adjusted, and measure unemployment as a share of the civilian labor force, not the total population. Thus, the indicators describe different time summaries. BLS estimates may be revised.

Sources: Census county population estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics—Maryland (catalog ID: DOL-BLS-3060-01).

Reproducibility evidence ID: e752f5ee513f48548f404b79e1e7098f.

Maryland counties ranked by population growth, 2023–2024

Ranked by percentage growth, using July 1 population estimates for both years from Census Vintage 2024. Baltimore city is included as a county equivalent.

Method: Growth = 100 × (2024 population − 2023 population) ÷ 2023 population. Rankings use unrounded rates, so displayed ties may have different ranks. These are estimates from a single vintage, not a ranking by the number of residents added.

U1 run 1 · 9.4 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: What was county advertising spending relative to population?

Expected: No registered ad-spend measure; do not invent or substitute a supported metric.

Agent answer

Which county or state and year do you mean? Also, does “advertising spending” mean county-government advertising expenses or political campaign advertising?

“Relative to population” usually means advertising dollars per resident. However, the available analysis tools do not support advertising-spending comparisons; they currently support only Maryland population and unemployment for 2022–2024. Once you clarify the scope, I can look for relevant public spending datasets, but I can’t calculate that comparison with these tools.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct abstention

Correct unsupported-metric refusal plus useful clarifying questions; no promise to calculate ad spend.

Question: Compare California county population and annual-average unemployment rate changes from 2020 to 2024.

Expected: Explicit unsupported geography/period; discovery may still help.

Agent answer

I can’t produce the requested California county comparison with the available analysis tools. The compatibility check confirms they support only Maryland counties for 2022–2024, not California or a 2020 baseline. No California comparison was run.

The requested comparison would report:

  • Population change: percent change from July 1, 2020 to July 1, 2024, using a consistent estimate vintage.
  • Annual-average unemployment-rate change: the 2024 rate minus the 2020 rate, in percentage points, not percent.

Relevant federal sources are:

  • Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — source ID census_population.
  • BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics — catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01. The verified tool snapshot contains Maryland data, not the California observations needed here.

July 1 population estimates and annual-average unemployment rates describe different temporal summaries; unemployment uses the civilian labor force, not total population, as its denominator. Any relationship between their changes would be descriptive, not evidence of causation.

I can instead run the supported Maryland 2022–2024 comparison.

U3 run 1 · 19.0 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

The available data do not establish that population growth caused unemployment to fall. That is not the same as proving population growth had no effect.

Since you did not specify dates, I checked 2022–2024, the supported period, comparing population with the unemployment rate, not the number of unemployed people.

Across Maryland’s 24 county-level jurisdictions, including Baltimore city, the comparison summary shows:

  • Population increased in 21.
  • Unemployment rates fell in 1, rose in 18, and were unchanged in 5.
  • Among the 21 growing jurisdictions, unemployment fell in 1, rose in 15, and was unchanged in 5.

Thus, population growth generally did not coincide with falling county unemployment rates over this period. These direction counts do not establish what happened to Maryland’s statewide aggregate rate, nor do they identify a causal effect.

There are also important measurement differences: population is a July 1 estimate, while unemployment is an annual-average, not-seasonally-adjusted rate whose denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population.

Bottom line: this descriptive comparison does not support the proposed causal conclusion; determining causation would require evidence beyond these two indicators.

Sources: Census county population estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01. Reproducible analysis evidence: efa4f3543efb409793922e92a5bf5c94.

Your score

U3 run 2 · 18.0 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

The available data do not establish that population growth caused Maryland unemployment to fall. That is not proof that population growth had no effect.

Since you did not specify dates, I checked 2022–2024, the supported comparison period. The comparison summary covers all 24 Maryland county-equivalents, including Baltimore city, with no missing coverage:

  • Population increased in 21.
  • Among those 21, unemployment rates rose in 15, fell in 1, and were unchanged in 5.
  • Across all 24, unemployment rates rose in 18, fell in 1, and were unchanged in 5.

Thus, population growth generally did not coincide with falling county unemployment rates over this period. These county counts do not establish whether Maryland’s statewide aggregate rate fell.

Important limits: This is a descriptive comparison, not a causal analysis. Population measures are July 1 estimates; unemployment measures are annual averages, not seasonally adjusted. Unemployment rates use the civilian labor force, not total population, as their denominator.

Sources: Census county population estimates, Vintage 2024 and BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01). Reproducible analysis evidence ID: 8e35e6eab8564ab7b4f78762614d9229.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI-assisted review; not human certification)

Overall: correct abstention

Independent numeric checks passed; evidence retrieved and model reported completed. Correctly distinguishes unproven causation from proof of no effect; no invented statewide aggregate.

U3 run 3 · 18.5 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

The available evidence does not establish that population growth caused unemployment to fall. For 2022–2024, the period supported by this analysis, the county pattern also does not show unemployment generally falling alongside population growth.

The comparison summary covers all 24 Maryland county-equivalents, including Baltimore city, with no missing coverage:

  • Population grew in 21.
  • Among those 21, unemployment rates rose in 15, fell in 1, and were unchanged in 5.
  • Across all 24, unemployment rates rose in 18, fell in 1, and were unchanged in 5.

These are county direction counts, not Maryland’s statewide unemployment rate; they do not establish whether the statewide rate fell.

More importantly, this is a descriptive comparison—not a causal study. It cannot isolate population growth’s effect from other influences or rule out reverse causality. That means causation is unproven, not that population growth had no effect.

Measurement caveats: Population is measured using July 1 estimates from Census Vintage 2024; unemployment is an annual-average, not-seasonally-adjusted rate. Its denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population. Population changes are measured in percent, while unemployment-rate changes are in percentage points.

Sources: Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, catalog ID DOL-BLS-3060-01.
Reproducible analysis evidence ID: b75f39b48e0d4b46a00e7e3291ba6fd8.
